Definition 2.45
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
be two
C^{*}
-algebras.
A
*
-homorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras

from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a bounded (i.e. continuous) linear map

f:{{\mathcal{B}}}\to{{\mathcal{A}}}

such that

i.

f(b^{*})=f(b)^{*}
,

ii.

f(b_{1}b_{2})=f(b_{1})f(b_{2}),
and

iii.

f(1_{{{\mathcal{B}}}})=1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}

for all
b,b_{1},b_{2}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}.

Remark 2.46
.

The word bounded in the definition of a
*
-homomorphism
of
C^{*}
-algebras is redundant.
It is a theorem that such a map satisfying
conditions i., ii., and iii. is automatically bounded
[
11
]
.
Thus, the reader may safely ignore that condition.

There are a few basic examples of
C^{*}
-algebras that we will focus on in
this section.

Example 2.47
.

{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a
C^{*}
-algebra with its usual structure associated with
complex numbers.
The involution
*
is the complex conjugate.
This is an example of a commutative
C^{*}
-algebra.

This previous example automatically induces a huge class
of examples.

Example 2.48
.

Let
X
be a finite set and let
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
denote the set of all
functions
X\to{{\mathbb{C}}}.
There is a unique algebra structure and involution
on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
so that the evaluation functions
{{\mathrm{ev}}}_{x}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}\to{{\mathbb{C}}},

defined by sending
\varphi:X\to{{\mathbb{C}}}
to
\varphi(x),

are
*
-homomorphisms for all
x\in X

(these are just the pointwise algebraic structures).
2
2

2

This way of phrasing the natural algebraic structure on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}

makes precise in what sense it is the “obvious” algebraic structure.
Note that
X
does not have to be finite for this statement.
However, one must be careful about the topology when
X
is
infinite—demanding the smallest topology for which
{{\mathrm{ev}}}_{x}

are all continuous would result in the topology of pointwise convergence,
which is not the one we will use later.

The norm on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
is given by

{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}\ni f\mapsto\lVert f\rVert:=\sup_{x\in X}\{|f(x)|\}.

(2.49)

The previous examples were commutative
C^{*}
-algebras.
The following example is the quintessential
C^{*}
-algebra
of quantum mechanics of a finite system (such as a qubit).

Example 2.50
.

Fix
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}}.
The set
M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})

of
n\times n
complex-valued matrices is a
C^{*}
-algebra with
product given by matrix multiplication and
*
is the transpose
complex conjugate. The norm can be taken to be
either the operator norm or the standard Euclidean norm
(the topology will be the same so it does not matter).

It is a theorem that every finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebra
is (
*
-isomorphic to) a direct sum of these (Theorem III.1.1 in
[
10
]
).

Example 2.51
.

More generally, for any
Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}},
the set of bounded operators
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
on

{{\mathcal{H}}}
is a
C^{*}
-algebra via operator composition,
*
given by the adjoint,
and the norm given by the operator norm.

Notation 2.52
.

Let
\mathbf{C\text{*-}Alg},

\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg},
and
\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}Alg}
denote the categories whose
objects are
C^{*}
-algebras, commutative
C^{*}
-algebras,
and finite-dimensional commutative
C^{*}
-algebras, respectively,
and whose the morphisms are all
*
-homomorphisms of
C^{*}
-algebras.

Definition 2.53
.

Given a
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}},

a
state
on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a
bounded (i.e. continuous)
3
3

3

The word bounded/continuous here is redundant.
It is a theorem that such a map on a
C^{*}
-algebra satisfying these
conditions is automatically bounded.

linear function
{\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}}
such that

\omega(1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}})=1
and
\omega(a^{*}a)\geq 0
for all
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

The following theorem relates states to density matrices and motivates
why density matrices are the states of quantum mechanics
as opposed to vectors (really, rays) in a Hilbert space
[
20
]
.

Theorem 2.54
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between
states on
M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and matrices
\rho\in M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})

satisfying
{\rm tr}(\rho)=1
and
\rho^{*}=\rho

(such matrices are known as
density matrices
).
More precisely, for any density matrix
\rho,
the function

{\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;):M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\to{{\mathbb{C}}},
sending
A\in M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
to

{\rm tr}(\rho A),
is a state. Conversely, for
any state
\omega:M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\to{{\mathbb{C}}},
there exists a unique density matrix

\rho
such that
\omega={\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;).

This theorem extends to states on
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
with
{{\mathcal{H}}}
a separable
Hilbert space
and states that satisfy an additional continuity assumption.
4
4

4

Such states go by the name of
normal states
for those readers
who would like to look up more information. However, normal states
require a certain notion of continuity not available on arbitrary

C^{*}
-algebras, and one requires the notion of a von Neumann algebra,
a special type of
C^{*}
-algebra that is closed with respect to a certain topology

[
36
]
.

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
is an example of a von Neumann algebra.

In the context of quantum mechanics,
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
is the set of
physical observables including non self-adjoint operators,
such as ladder operators, so that it remains closed under
multiplication by complex numbers.
A state on
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
assigns to every observable a number,
interpreted as the expectation value of that observable
for that state.
States on
C^{*}
-algebras and their relationship to the more standard
approach of quantum mechanics in terms of Hilbert spaces is
described in a categorical framework in
[
32
]
.

2.4
Two types of morphisms of
C^{*}
-algebras

There is another notion of a morphism that is important
for
C^{*}
-algebras and utilizes less of its structure.

Definition 2.55
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. An element
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
is

positive
iff there exists an element
b\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
such that

a=b^{*}b.
Positivity of elements induces a partial ordering on
{{\mathcal{A}}}

and the notation
a\geq 0
will be used to indicate that
a
is positive
(
b\geq a
iff
b-a\geq 0
).

Definition 2.56
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
be two
C^{*}
-algebras.
A
positive (unital) map
from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a
bounded linear function
\varphi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\to{{\mathcal{A}}}
such that

\varphi(1_{{{\mathcal{B}}}})=1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
and
\varphi(b^{*}b)\geq 0
for all
b\in{{\mathcal{B}}}.

A state is an example of a positive map.
Every
*
-homomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras is a positive map.
The converse, however, is far from true.
Examples are given in Section
2.7
.
Non-unital positive maps seem to be the weakest structure
needed to make sense of operations and processes
on quantum mechanical systems though one may also relax the
unit-preserving condition
[
25
]
.

Notation 2.57
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be an involutive algebra and let
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}}.

M_{n}({{\mathcal{A}}})
is the set of of
n\times n
matrices with
coefficients in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
with product given by matrix multiplication
and
*
given by the transpose and applying
*
in each entry.

Theorem 2.58
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra.
Then there exists a unique norm
\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert
on
M_{n}({{\mathcal{A}}})

such that

\lVert AB\rVert\leq\lVert A\rVert\lVert B\rVert\qquad\forall\;A,B\in M_{n}({{\mathcal{A}}})

(2.59)

and

\lVert A^{*}A\rVert=\lVert A\rVert^{2}\qquad\forall\;A\in M_{n}({{\mathcal{A}}}).

(2.60)

Furthermore,
M_{n}({{\mathcal{A}}})
with this structure is a
C^{*}
-algebra.

Proof.

See Chapter 1 of Paulsen
[
33
]

or Chapters 4 and 6 of Warner
[
49
]
.
∎

Remark 2.61
.

There are
*
-isomorphisms

M_{n}({{\mathcal{A}}})\cong M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes{{\mathcal{A}}}\cong{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

(2.62)

Therefore, all of these involutive algebras have a unique norm
giving them the structure of
C^{*}
-algebras.
The theory of tensor products of
C^{*}
-algebras is notoriously complicated.
Since we will work with commutative
C^{*}
-algebras for the most part, we
will avoid such complications.

Definition 2.63
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
be two
C^{*}
-algebras.
A
completely positive (unital) map
from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a
function
\varphi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\to{{\mathcal{A}}}
such that

\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n}:{{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\to{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})

is a positive map for all
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}}.
The abbreviation “CP map” will often be used
for such maps.

Remark 2.64
.

One motivation for this definition comes from
quantum mechanics
[
27
]
,
[
25
]
.
The concept of tensoring with the algebra

M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and the positive map with the identity
means to adjoin another finite system that does not interact
with the original system on
{{\mathcal{B}}}
during the operation. The resulting map

\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n}:{{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\to{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes M_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})

should therefore still be positive. There are examples of positive
maps that are not completely positive so requiring this condition to be
satisfied is an additional constraint
[
33
]
,
[
25
]
.
Unital maps of
C^{*}
-algebras correspond to the trace-preserving
condition that is often referred to in texts on quantum information

[
30
]
. See Example
2.93
for an illustration.
All maps in this work are unital so we will not use the
more standard notation “CPU”
[
30
]
.

The composition of CP maps is CP
[
33
]
.

Theorem 2.65
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra.
Every positive map
{{\mathcal{A}}}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}
is CP.
In particular, a state on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is CP.
Furthermore, every positive map between commutative
C^{*}
-algebras
is CP.

Proof.

See Theorems 3.9 and 3.11 in
[
33
]
.
∎

Notation 2.66
.

Henceforth, a
*
-homomorphism from a
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to
another
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
will be denoted by a
straight arrow
{{\mathcal{B}}}\to{{\mathcal{A}}}.
A CP map,
on the other hand, will be denoted with a curvy arrow

{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{A}}}.

We now collect all the categories of
C^{*}
-algebras that will be needed
in the prequel.

Notation 2.67
.

Let
\mathbf{C\text{*-}AlgCP},

\mathbf{cC\text{*-}AlgPos},
and
\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}AlgPos}
denote the categories whose
objects are
C^{*}
-algebras, commutative
C^{*}
-algebras,
and finite-dimensional commutative
C^{*}
-algebras, respectively,
and whose the morphisms are all CP maps of
C^{*}
-algebras
(note that in the latter two cases, CP maps are the same as
positive maps).

On a given physical system corresponding to some
C^{*}
-algebra,
a state provides expectation values for observables.
One can define a category of
C^{*}
-algebras equipped with a state
together with the various flavors of morphisms analogous to the case
of finite probability. However, this is tangential to our main goal
and will follow as an immediate consequence anyway.
Examples are provided in Section
2.7
, but first
we connect algebra with probability theory.

2.5
From probability theory to algebra

Now, we will construct a functor

C:\mathbf{FinSetStoch}\to\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}AlgPos}
and show that it restricts to a well-defined
functor
C:\mathbf{FinSetFun}\to\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}Alg}.
The same notation for these functors will
be used as it will be clear from context which one is being referred to.
The properties of these functors will be discussed
in Section
2.6
.
Associated to each finite set
Y,
one has the
C^{*}
-algebra

{{{\mathbb{C}}}}^{Y}
discussed in Example
2.48
.
Note that any element
v\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}
can be uniquely expressed as

v=\sum_{y\in Y}v_{y}e_{y},

(2.68)

where
e_{y}:Y\to{{\mathbb{C}}}
is defined by

Y\ni y^{\prime}\mapsto e_{y}(y^{\prime}):=\delta_{yy^{\prime}}

(2.69)

and
v_{y}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}
is given by
v(y)=:v_{y}.

Hence, if
X
is another finite set,
any CP map
\varphi:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}

is determined by its value on the set of elements
\{e_{y}\}_{y\in Y}
by linearity.
Namely,

\varphi(v)=\sum_{y\in Y}v_{y}\varphi(e_{y}).

(2.70)

Hence, to every stochastic map
f:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y,
associate the linear function

C^{f}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}

uniquely determined by

C^{f}(e_{y}):=\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}e_{x}

(2.71)

for all
y\in Y.

These assignments define the functor
C.

Theorem 2.72
.

The assignment
5
5

5

The
\mathrm{op}
superscript is only meant to keep track that the
directionality of the arrows get reversed. This is shown in
(
2.71
), where the domain of
C^{f}
is
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}
instead of

{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}.
The directionality change is also shown in (
2.77
).

\begin{split}\mathbf{FinSetStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}&\xrightarrow{C}\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}AlgPos}\\
X&\mapsto{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}\\
\Big(f:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y\Big)&\mapsto\Big(C^{f}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}\Big),\end{split}

(2.73)

where
C^{f}
is defined in (
2.71
),
is a well-defined functor.

Proof.

The proof will be broken into three steps.
It will be shown that

C^{f}
is CP,
the identity stochastic map goes to the identity CP map,
and the composition of stochastic maps gets sent
to the composition of CP maps.

i.

By Theorem
2.65
,
it suffices to show that
C^{f}
is positive.
Every positive element in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}
is of the form

\sum_{y\in Y}a_{y}e_{y},\qquad\text{with }\;a_{y}\geq 0\quad\forall\;y\in Y.

(2.74)

Hence, by linearity, it suffices to show that
C^{f}(e_{y}),

given by (
2.71
),
is positive for every
y\in Y.
By definition of
f
being a stochastic
map,
f_{yx}\geq 0
for all
y\in Y
and
x\in X.
Hence,

C^{f}(e_{y})
is a positive element in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}.

Furthermore,
C^{f}
is unital because

C^{f}(1_{Y})=C^{f}\left(\sum_{y\in Y}e_{y}\right)=\sum_{y\in Y}\big(C^{f}(e_{y})\big)=\sum_{y\in Y}\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}e_{x}=\sum_{x\in X}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{y\in Y}f_{yx}\right)}_{1}e_{x}=1_{X}.

(2.75)

ii.

The identity function
\mathrm{id}:X\to X
has the corresponding stochastic map
given by the probability measure
\mathrm{id}_{x^{\prime}x}=\delta_{x^{\prime}x}
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X.

Therefore,

C^{\mathrm{id}}(e_{x^{\prime}})=\sum_{x\in X}\mathrm{id}_{x^{\prime}x}e_{x^{\prime}}=\sum_{x\in X}\delta_{x^{\prime}x}e_{x}=e_{x^{\prime}}

(2.76)

for all
x^{\prime}\in X.
Thus,
C^{\mathrm{id}}
is the identity CP map.

iii.

Now suppose that
f:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y
and
g:Y\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Z
are two
composable stochastic maps between finite sets.
This induces CP maps

C^{f}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}

and
C^{g}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Z}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}.

Therefore,

\begin{split}C^{f}\big(C^{g}(e_{z})\big)&=C^{f}\left(\sum_{y\in Y}g_{zy}e_{y}\right)\\
&=\sum_{y\in Y}g_{zy}C^{f}(e_{y})\\
&=\sum_{y\in Y}g_{zy}\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}e_{x}\\
&=\sum_{x\in X}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{y\in Y}g_{zy}f_{yx}\right)}_{(g\circ f)_{zx}}e_{x}\\
&=C^{g\circ f}(e_{z})\end{split}

(2.77)

for all
z\in Z.

∎

Theorem 2.78
.

The functor
C:\mathbf{FinSetStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}AlgPos}
from Theorem

2.72
restricts to
a well-defined functor
C:\mathbf{FinSetFun}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}Alg}.

Proof.

Using the notation of Theorem
2.72
and its proof,
all that is left to show is that
C^{f}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
is a

*
-homomorphism when
f
is a function. By linearity, it suffices to prove that

C^{f}(e_{y}e_{y^{\prime}})=C^{f}(e_{y})C^{f}(e_{y^{\prime}})

for all
y,y^{\prime}\in Y.
This follows from
6
6

6

Diagrams like this are to be read from top to bottom going along
either direction to recreate the thought-process involved in proving the claim.
The reason is because at some point near the bottom, a step which does not
seem obvious in one direction is perhaps more intuitive from the other
direction (namely,
\delta_{yf(x)}^{2}=\delta_{yf(x)}
).

\textstyle{\displaystyle C^{f}(e_{y})C^{f}(e_{y^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{x,x^{\prime}\in X}\delta_{yf(x)}\delta_{y^{\prime}f(x^{\prime})}e_{x}e_{x^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{x,x^{\prime}\in X}\delta_{yf(x)}\delta_{y^{\prime}f(x^{\prime})}\delta_{xx^{\prime}}e_{x^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\;\;\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\delta_{yf(x)}\delta_{y^{\prime}f(x)}e_{x}}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\delta_{yy^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in X}\delta_{yf(x)}^{2}e_{x}}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\delta_{yy^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in X}\delta_{yf(x)}e_{x}\;\;}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\delta_{yy^{\prime}}C^{f}(e_{y})}
\textstyle{\displaystyle C^{f}(\delta_{yy^{\prime}}e_{y})}
\textstyle{\displaystyle C^{f}(e_{y}e_{y^{\prime}})}

(2.79)

∎

2.6
From algebra to probability theory

We will show that the functors of Theorems

2.72
and
2.78
are fully faithful.

Definition 2.80
.

A functor
F:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{E}
from a category
\mathcal{D}
to a category
\mathcal{E}

is
fully faithful
iff for every pair of objects
d
and
d^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D},

the function
F:\mathcal{D}(d,d^{\prime})\to\mathcal{E}\big(F(d),F(d^{\prime})\big)
is a bijection
(
full
means this function is surjective and
faithful

means it is injective). Here,
\mathcal{D}(d,d^{\prime})
denotes the set of morphisms
from
d
to
d^{\prime}
in the category
\mathcal{D}.

In other words, if there is a fully faithful functor
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{E}
that
is also injective on objects, this allows one to study

\mathcal{D}
in a broader context without altering the relations between its objects.
Being faithful means that no information is lost when viewing

\mathcal{D}
as inside of
\mathcal{E}
and being full means that no extra information is added to
\mathcal{D}

when viewing
\mathcal{D}
inside of
\mathcal{E}.
In particular,
\mathcal{D}
when viewed on its own
has the same set (if it even is a set) of isomorphism classes of objects
as when viewing
\mathcal{D}
inside of
\mathcal{E}.

Theorem 2.81
.

The functors
C:\mathbf{FinSetStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}AlgPos}
and

C:\mathbf{FinSetFun}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}Alg}

from Theorems
2.72
and
2.78

are fully faithful and injective on objects.

Proof.

Injectivity on objects is immediate from the definition of
C.

Fix any two sets
X
and
Y

and a CP map
\varphi:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}.

The goal is to show
there exists a unique stochastic map
f:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y

such that
{C}^{f}=\varphi.

Furthermore, when

\varphi
is a
*
-homomorphism, then the associated stochastic map

f
is a function. These two claims are proven in two steps.

i.

First note that for any
y\in Y,

\varphi(e_{y})
is an element of
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}

and, because
\{e_{x}\}_{x\in X}
is a basis of
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X},

there exist unique numbers
\{f_{yx}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}\}_{x\in X}
such that

\varphi(e_{y})=\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}e_{x}.

(2.82)

Positivity of
\varphi
demands that

f_{yx}\geq 0

(2.83)

for all
x\in X
(and
y\in Y
).
Since
\varphi
is linear and unital,

\hskip-2.84526pt\sum_{x\in X}e_{x}=1_{X}=\varphi(1_{Y})=\varphi\left(\sum_{y\in Y}e_{y}\right)=\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(e_{y})=\sum_{y\in Y}\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}e_{x}=\sum_{x\in X}\left(\sum_{y\in Y}f_{yx}\right)e_{x}.

(2.84)

Since
\{e_{x}\}_{x\in X}
is a linearly independent set of vectors in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X},

this implies

\sum_{y\in Y}f_{yx}=1.

(2.85)

This shows that for every
x\in X,
the assignment

Y\ni y\mapsto f_{yx}

(2.86)

is a probability measure on
Y.
In other words, the numbers

\{f_{yx}\}_{y\in Y,x\in X}
define a stochastic map
f:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y.

It is readily checked that
{C}^{f}=\varphi.
This proves that

{C}
is full.

To see that
C
is faithful, suppose that

f^{\prime}:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y
is another stochastic map such that

{C}^{f}={C}^{f^{\prime}}.
In particular, this implies that
for any
y\in Y,

\textstyle{{C}^{f}(e_{y})}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}e_{x}}
\textstyle{{C}^{f^{\prime}}(e_{y})}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}f^{\prime}_{yx}e_{x}}

(2.87)

By linear independence of the set
\{e_{x}\}_{x\in X},
this implies

f_{yx}=f^{\prime}_{yx}\qquad\forall\;x\in X.

(2.88)

Since this calculation was independent of
y,
this also proves the equality
for all
y\in Y.
Hence,
f=f^{\prime}
proving that
{C}
is faithful.
This concludes the proof that
{C}
is fully faithful.

ii.

Using the same notation as in the previous step, it suffices to show that
if
\varphi:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
is actually a
*
-homomorphism, then the
corresponding stochastic map

f:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y
comes from a function
f:X\to Y.

First notice that for any pair of
distinct

elements
y,y^{\prime}\in Y,
because
\varphi
is a homomorphism,

\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in X}f_{yx}f_{y^{\prime}x^{\prime}}\delta_{xx^{\prime}}e_{x^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}f_{y^{\prime}x}e_{x}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\varphi(0)}
\textstyle{\varphi(e_{y}e_{y^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{\varphi(e_{y})\varphi(e_{y^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in X}f_{yx}f_{y^{\prime}x^{\prime}}e_{x}e_{x^{\prime}}}

(2.89)

proving that

f_{yx}f_{y^{\prime}x}=0\qquad\forall\;x\in X.

(2.90)

Putting this result aside for the time being,
recall the result (
2.85
) which, together with the fact that

f_{yx}\geq 0
for all
y\in Y
and
x\in X,
implies that for each
x\in X,

there exists a
y\in Y
such that
f_{yx}>0
(for otherwise, the sum
over all
y
would not be
1
). For any other
y^{\prime}\in Y\setminus\{y\},

the result (
2.90
) then implies

f_{y^{\prime}x}=0\qquad\forall\;y^{\prime}\in Y\setminus\{y\}.

(2.91)

Using (
2.85
) again implies

f_{yx}=1.

(2.92)

In other words, for every
x\in X,
there exists a unique
y\in Y

such that
f_{yx}=1
and
f_{y^{\prime}x}=0
for all
y^{\prime}\in Y\setminus\{y\}.

Set
f(x)
to be this unique element
y,
i.e.
f(x):=y.

This assignment then defines a function
f:X\to Y.

∎

It is a fact that every
n
-dimensional commutative
C^{*}
-algebra is

*
-isomorphic to
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
for some set
X
with cardinality
n

(this will follow from results in Section
3
).
This, together with
Theorem
2.81
, says that
\mathbf{FinSetStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}
is equivalent
to the category
\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}AlgPos}
consisting of finite-dimensional
commutative
C^{*}
-algebras and positive maps. An explicit inverse will
be constructed in greater generality in Section
4.5
.

2.7
Some quantum mechanics

Completely positive maps represent general processes that
are allowed in quantum mechanics. We will provide
three different examples illustrating the versatility of the

C^{*}
-algebraic approach.
The first two examples are actually examples of
*
-homomorphisms.
We thank Stefan Andronache and Marcelo Nomura for discussions
leading to these examples.

Example 2.93
.

Let
{{\mathcal{H}}}
be a finite-dimensional
7
7

7

We use finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces
to avoid technicalities involving domains of operators.

Hilbert space and
H
a self-adjoint
operator, thought of as a Hamiltonian. For each
t\in{{\mathbb{R}}},
the operator

\exp\left(-\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right),
where
\hbar
is the reduced Planck constant,
is unitary and describes unitary time evolution in quantum mechanics.
Its action on observables is given by the adjoint action
(the Heisenberg picture)

\begin{split}{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})&\to{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})\\
A&\mapsto\exp\left(\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right)A\exp\left(-\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right).\end{split}

(2.94)

Infinitesimally, this gives the differential equation that describes
the time evolution of an observable

\frac{dA}{dt}=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[A,H].

(2.95)

Now, given an initial state
{\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;):{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}

for some density matrix
\rho

(see Theorem
2.54
for notation) this pulls back
to a new state

{\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;)\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\exp\left(-\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right)}

under this evolution

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}_{\exp\left(-\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;)}
\scriptstyle{{\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;)\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\exp\left(-\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right)}}

(2.96)

and is given by the assignment

\begin{split}{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})&\to{{\mathbb{C}}}\\
A&\mapsto{\rm tr}\left(\rho\exp\left(\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right)A\exp\left(-\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right)\right)={\rm tr}\left(\exp\left(-\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right)\rho\exp\left(\frac{itH}{\hbar}\right)A\right)\end{split}

(2.97)

by cyclicity of the trace. By the uniqueness of density matrices representing
states (Theorem
2.54
),
this proves that the time evolution of
\rho
is given by the
differential equation

\frac{d\rho}{dt}=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H,\rho].

(2.98)

This is the quantum Liouville equation. Notice how it has the opposite sign
of the evolution for observables. Also notice that if
\rho
is a pure state,
this reduces to Schrödinger’s equation and reproduces the Schrödinger
picture. Thus, the
C^{*}
-algebraic approach naturally incorporates both
perspectives. It is important to notice that the straight horizontal arrow in
(
2.96
) indicates
deterministic
evolution!
The “non-determinacy” of this example
only appears in the states and their expectation values.

Example 2.99
.

Measurement is an example of a CP map, in fact a

*
-homomorphism.
We think of measuring a quantum mechanical system
in terms of numbers. For example, in the Stern-Gerlach experiment,
we measure a particle (such as a silver atom) to be spin up or spin
down depending on its position on a screen after moving through
a magnetic field
[
41
]
. A state of a quantum-mechanical system is
therefore reduced to a probability measure on the set
of the eigenvalues of the observable being measured.

The general (finite-dimensional) situation is as follows.
8
8

8

For infinite systems, one must use projection-valued
measures
[
30
]
.

Let
{{\mathcal{H}}}
be a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, let

A
be a self-adjoint operator on
{{\mathcal{H}}},
and let
\omega
be a state
on
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}).

Let
\sigma(A)
denote the spectrum of
A
(in this case,

\sigma(A)
is just the set of eigenvalues of
A
).

A
is an observable and
\sigma(A)
are the possible values
that an observer will see when trying to measure the observable
A.

It is a fact of life that an observer can
only
measure elements
of
\sigma(A)
(this is the origin of the “discreteness” of quantum mechanics).
In this situation, what is the probability measure that
an observer expects to see on
\sigma(A)
?
The resulting probability measure is obtained by pulling back
\omega

along the “measurement” map

\begin{split}m:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}&\to{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})\\
e_{\lambda}&\mapsto P_{\lambda},\end{split}

(2.100)

where
P_{\lambda}
is the projection operator onto the eigenspace associated
with the eigenvalue
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
of
A.

This produces a state on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}
via pullback

\textstyle{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{m}
\scriptstyle{\omega\circ m}
\scriptstyle{\omega}

(2.101)

By our analysis from Section
2.6
,
p:=\omega\circ m

precisely provides us with a probability measure
on
\sigma(A).
This probability measure is interpreted as the
probability distribution of measuring the corresponding eigenvalues
of
A
with respect to the state
\omega.

Note that this example does not have an evolution of the form

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})\to{{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathcal{K})
between observables on Hilbert spaces

{{\mathcal{H}}}
and
\mathcal{K}.

Instead, it is an “evolution” from the quantum phase space
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})

to the classical one
\sigma(A).

Hence, the
C^{*}
-algebraic formulation allows one to treat classical
and quantum systems in the same category.

In both of the previous situations, the evolution was described by
a
*
-homomorphism. In the following example, we provide
completely positive evolution that is not a
*
-homomorphism.
Such evolutions can be used to model noise and measurement
among other things
[
30
]
.

Example 2.102
.

Let
\big\{|\!\uparrow\rangle,|\!\downarrow\rangle\big\}
denote the standard Euclidean basis in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}.

Let
|\!\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\!|
denote the projection operator onto

\mathrm{span}\big(|\!\uparrow\rangle\big)
and similarly for
|\!\downarrow\rangle.

The states on
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{2})
given by
{\rm tr}\big(|\!\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\!|\;\cdot\;\big)

and
{\rm tr}\big(|\!\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\!|\;\cdot\;\big)
can be interpreted as a qubit
being either in the spin up or spin down state with respect to an
appropriately chosen coordinate frame. Consider an operation on the
space of all density matrices whose action on these two states is given by

\begin{split}|\!\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\!|&\mapsto p|\!\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\!|+(1-p)|\!\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\!|\\
|\!\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\!|&\mapsto(1-p)|\!\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\!|+p|\!\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\!|\end{split}

(2.103)

for some fixed
p\in[0,1].
This is to be interpreted as an operation that
has probability
1-p
of flipping a spin up state to a spin down state
and has probability
1-p
of flipping a spin down state to a spin up state.
Does there exist a completely positive map
f:{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{2})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{2})

that pulls back the pure states above on the left
in (
2.103
) to the states on the right in (
2.103
)?
The answer is yes. Let

E:=\sqrt{p}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\qquad\&\qquad F:=\sqrt{1-p}\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}

(2.104)

and define
f
to be given by

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{2})\ni A\mapsto E^{*}AE+F^{*}AF.

(2.105)

One can check that
f
is completely positive and is not a

*
-homomorphism. The induced action on the density matrices
can be obtained by looking at the pullback of an initial density matrix
\rho

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{2})\ni A\mapsto{\rm tr}\big(\rho f(A)\big)={\rm tr}(\rho E^{*}AE+\rho F^{*}AF)={\rm tr}\big((E\rho E^{*}+F\rho F^{*})A\big).

(2.106)

Hence, the resulting density matrix under such an operation is given by

E\rho E^{*}+F\rho F^{*}.
One can check that
(
2.103
) holds under this transformation.
So again, the
C^{*}
-algebraic framework
is able to reproduce familiar operations such as this one, known as the
bit flip channel
[
30
]
, all in the same category of
physical processes.

A generalization of Example
2.93

to CP maps gives rise to Lindblad’s equation describing

non-deterministic
dynamics in (open) quantum systems

[
27
]
.

3
An equivalence between spaces and algebraic structures

On their own, the study of topological spaces and continuous maps
is quite different from the study of algebra and homomorphisms.
Topological spaces consist
of sets equipped with a subset of the power set (known as open sets)
and continuous maps
are functions whose pre-image function takes open sets to open sets.
Algebras are sets equipped with binary operations satisfying certain
laws such as associativity and maps between algebras are those
that respect this algebraic structure.
In the plain context of set theory, it simply does not make sense to compare
the two areas of mathematics. However, viewing these two areas

as a whole
, which is achieved by viewing them as categories,
it makes sense to compare them.
In fact, under additional suitable restrictions on the respective sides,
the two categories are
equivalent
.
There are several other versions of such equivalences between
topological categories and algebraic ones
[
23
]
,
[
22
]
.
We will discuss one such equivalence, the equivalence
between the category of commutative (unital)
C^{*}
-algebras and
*
-homomorphisms
with the category of compact Hausdorff topological spaces and continuous
maps following the expositions in Folland
[
13
]
, Rudin
[
39
]
,
and Tao’s online notes
[
47
]
.
The references already have well-written proofs of the main results.
Our purpose here is to frame these results in the appropriate
categorical context and establish certain tools that will be used in
Section
4
when we relax the class
of morphisms to include non-deterministic processes.

3.1
From spaces to algebras

In Section
2.5
, we showed how given a finite set
X,

the set of functions
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
can be made into a
C^{*}
-algebra
in a natural manner. This can also be done if
X
is replaced
with a compact Hausdorff space and the function space is chosen
to be the subspace of
continuous
functions from
X
to
{{\mathbb{C}}}.

Example 3.1
.

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space.
Then the space of complex-valued continuous
functions, denoted by
C(X),
with the
algebraic structure from
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}

and with norm given by the uniform norm, i.e.

C(X)\ni\varphi\mapsto\lVert\varphi\rVert:=\sup_{x\in X}|\varphi(x)|,

(3.2)

is a commutative
C^{*}
-algebra.
Cauchy completeness follows from the continuous (uniform) limit theorem
(see Theorem 7.12 in
[
37
]
or Theorem 21.6 in
[
29
]
).
The constant function
1_{X}
is the unit for
C(X).

Note that although compactness is needed for
C(X)
to be a

C^{*}
-algebra with this norm, it is not necessary that
X
be Hausdorff.
Proving that
C(X)
is a
C^{*}
-algebra under the only assumption that

X
is compact is a good exercise.
9
9

9

Sketch of proof: start with a Cauchy sequence
in
C(X),
use Cauchy completeness in
{{\mathbb{C}}}
to obtain a candidate function
pointwise,
show that the resulting function is bounded, then show that the sequence
of functions converges to it uniformly, and finally apply the uniform limit theorem
to guarantee it is continuous.

If
X
is not Hausdorff, there exist points that cannot be separated
by continuous complex valued functions so that continuous functions
cannot distinguish them (see Remark
3.65

and Theorem
4.102
for further details).

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
X
and
Y
be compact Hausdorff spaces and let

f:X\to Y
be a continuous function. The function
C^{f}:C(Y)\to C(X)

defined by

C(Y)\ni\beta\mapsto C^{f}(\varphi):=\varphi\circ f

(3.4)

is a
*
-homomorphism.

Proof.

First note that
C^{f}(\varphi)
is continuous since it is the composition
of continuous functions.
To see that
C^{f}
respects the
product, let
\psi,\varphi\in C(Y)
and let
x\in X.
Then

\begin{split}\big(C^{f}(\psi\varphi)\big)(x)&=(\psi\varphi)\big(f(x)\big)\qquad\text{by def'n of $C^{f}$}\\
&=\Big(\psi\big(f(x)\big)\Big)\Big(\varphi\big(f(x)\big)\Big)\qquad\text{by def'n of $\psi\varphi$}\\
&=\big((\psi\circ f)(x)\big)\big((\varphi\circ f)(x)\big)\qquad\text{by def'n of $\circ$}\\
&=\big((\psi\circ f)(\varphi\circ f)\big)(x)\qquad\text{by def'n of $(\psi\circ f)(\varphi\circ f)$}\\
&=\big(C^{f}(\psi)C^{f}(\varphi)\big)(x)\qquad\text{by def'n of $C^{f}$}\end{split}

(3.5)

proving that
C^{f}(\psi\varphi)=C^{f}(\psi)C^{f}(\varphi).
Linearity,
*
-preservation,
and preservation of units follow from analogous calculations.
∎

Notation 3.6
.

Let
\mathbf{cHaus}
denote the category whose objects are compact Hausdorff
spaces and whose morphisms are continuous maps.

Proposition 3.7
.

The assignment

\begin{split}\mathbf{cHaus}^{\mathrm{op}}&\xrightarrow{C}\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}\\
X&\mapsto C(X)\\
\Big(X\xrightarrow{f}Y\Big)&\mapsto\Big(C(X)\xleftarrow{C^{f}}C(Y)\Big)\end{split}

(3.8)

from Example
3.1
and Proposition
3.3

defines a functor.

Proof.

From the definition of
C,
it is manifest that
C
is a functor:
the identity
\mathrm{id}_{X}:X\to X
gets sent to
C^{\mathrm{id}_{X}}=\mathrm{id}_{C(X)}

and the composition
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
gets sent
to the function

C(Z)\ni\varphi\mapsto C^{g\circ f}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ(g\circ f)=(\varphi\circ g)\circ f=C^{f}\big(\varphi\circ g\big)=C^{f}\big(C^{g}(\varphi)\big)=(C^{f}\circ C^{g})(\varphi)

(3.9)

since composition of functions is associative.
∎

3.2
Some topological preliminaries

Given a Banach algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}},
a certain subspace, denoted by
{{\mathcal{A}}}^{*},
of
the
linear
dual space
{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\vee}

has a natural topology (the operator norm topology)
coming from the norms on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathbb{C}}}.

However, this topology has too many open sets for some purposes.
Another topology on
{{\mathcal{A}}}^{*}
that has fewer open sets is the weak* topology.
Fewer open sets allows more sets to be compact and more compact
sets means that sequences and nets have a better chance of
converging (see Tao’s notes for an illuminating discussion
[
47
]
).
There are several ways to describe this topology. The simplest for our purposes
is in terms of convergent nets, though it will
also be useful to describe it in terms of topological
vector spaces whose topology is induced by a family of seminorms.
This latter perspective will also become more important in Section

4
.

Definition 3.10
.

A
seminorm
on a
vector space
V
is a function

\lVert\ \cdot\ \rVert:V\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
satisfying

i.

\lVert v\rVert\geq 0
for all
v\in V,

ii.

\lVert\lambda v\rVert=|\lambda|\lVert v\rVert
for all

\lambda\in{{\mathbb{C}}},v\in V,
and

iii.

\lVert v+w\rVert\leq\lVert v\rVert+\lVert w\rVert

for all
v,w\in V.

The only difference between a norm and a seminorm is that
a norm satisfies the additional condition that
\lVert v\rVert=0

implies
v=0.

Example 3.11
.

Let
V
be a vector space and
(W,\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert)
be a normed
vector space. For any linear map
\varphi:V\to W,
the function

\|\;\cdot\;\rVert_{\varphi}:V\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
defined by sending
v\in V

to
\big\lVert\varphi(v)\big\rVert
is a seminorm on
V.

A particular case of interest is when
W:={{\mathbb{C}}}.

A single norm provides a natural topology via
open balls of varying radii at different points.
While a single seminorm also provides a natural topology
in a similar way, it is rarely Hausdorff and does not enjoy
many desirable properties (see Example
3.15
).
However, it does become a topological
vector space.

Definition 3.12
.

A
topological vector space
consists of a vector
space
V
equipped with a topology in which
the addition of vectors
+:V\times V\to V
and
scalar multiplication
{{\mathbb{C}}}\times V\to V
are both continuous.

Definition 3.13
.

A
base
on a set
X
is
a collection
\mathfrak{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
of
subsets of
X
satisfying

i.

for each
x\in X,
there exists a

B\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
x\in B,

ii.

for every pair
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}
and
every
x\in B_{1}\cap B_{2},
there exists
a
B\in\mathfrak{B}
such that

x\in B\subseteq B_{1}\cap B_{2}.

A base
\mathfrak{B}
as above
can be used to construct a topology
on
X
by setting
\tau_{\mathfrak{B}}
to be the smallest
topology containing
\mathfrak{B}.
Explicitly, its elements
are given by unions of all possible elements in
\mathfrak{B},

\tau_{\mathfrak{B}}=\left\{\bigcup_{B\in\mathfrak{C}}B\;:\;\mathfrak{C}\subseteq\mathfrak{B}\right\}.

(3.14)

This topology is called the

topology generated
by
\mathfrak{B}.

Example 3.15
.

Let
\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert
be a seminorm on a vector space

V.
Then the set

\Big\{B_{r}(v)\subseteq V\;:\;v\in V\;\text{ and }\;r>0\Big\},

(3.16)

where

B_{r}(v):=\big\{w\in V\;:\;\lVert v-w\rVert<r\big\},

(3.17)

forms a base on
V.
With respect to the topology generated by this base,
V
is a
topological vector space. Furthermore, it is Hausdorff if and only if

\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert
is a norm (see Tao
[
47
]
).

B_{r}(v)
is called the
open ball
of radius
r\geq 0

centered at
v\in V.

A sufficiently robust

family
of seminorms typically has a more manageable topology
that naturally arises frequently enough to merit study.

Notation 3.18
.

For
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}},
set
\overline{n}:=\{1,\dots,n\}.
Let
X
be a set and

\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
a subset of the power set.
10
10

10

\mathfrak{S}
is a Fraktur “S.”

The value of a function
S:\overline{n}\to\mathfrak{S}
at
i\in\overline{n}
is
denoted by
S_{i}
instead of
S(i).

Definition 3.19
.

A
subbase
on a set
X
is
a collection
\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
of
subsets of
X
such that the set

\mathfrak{B}:=\bigcup_{n\in\{0\}\cup{{\mathbb{N}}}}\left\{\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}S_{i}\;:\;\overline{n}\xrightarrow{S}\mathfrak{S}\right\}

(3.20)

of all finite intersections of elements in
\mathfrak{S}
is a base
for
X.
Here,
\displaystyle\bigcap_{i=1}^{0}S_{i}
is the empty intersection,
which is taken to be
X
itself.
The
topology generated
by
\mathfrak{S}

is the topology generated by
\mathfrak{B}.

It is a fact that any collection of
subsets of a set forms a subbase for some topology on that set
and that topology is the smallest topology containing those subsets
(see Section 5 of
[
50
]
).

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
V
be a vector space with
\{\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
a
family of seminorms on
V
indexed by some set
A.

Let
\tau_{\alpha}
denote the topology
associated to
\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert_{\alpha}.

Then
\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}:=\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}\tau_{\alpha}

is a subbase on
V
and

V
with the topology generated by
\mathfrak{S}

is a topological vector space. Furthermore,
V
is
Hausdorff if and only if for each
v\in V\setminus\{0\},
there
exists an
\alpha\in A
such that
\lVert v\rVert_{\alpha}\neq 0.

Such a family of seminorms is said to
separate points
of
V

or is said to be a
separating family
.

Proof.

See Theorem 5.14 and Proposition 5.16 in Folland
[
14
]
.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

The use of seminorms is not necessary in the conclusion that
V

is a topological vector space. All that is required
is that each of the topologies
\tau_{\alpha}
provide
V
with the structure
of a topological vector space
[
47
]
.

Let
V
be a vector space and let
V^{\vee}
denote its linear dual
(linear functions from
V
to
{{\mathbb{C}}}
)
In many applications where
V
has a norm,

V^{\vee}
is too large to deal with. Instead, one uses the norm
to restrict to a certain subspace of linear functionals.

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
V
be a normed vector space with norm
\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert.

For
\chi\in V^{\vee},
set (abusing notation slightly)

\lVert\chi\rVert:=\inf\Big\{M\geq 0\;:\;\big|\chi(v)\big|\leq M\lVert v\rVert\quad\forall\;v\in V\Big\},

(3.24)

which can be infinite.
Set
V^{*}\subseteq V^{\vee}
to be the subset given by

V^{*}:=\big\{\chi\in V^{\vee}\;:\;\lVert\chi\rVert<\infty\big\}.

(3.25)

Then
\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert
is a norm on
V^{*}
and
V^{*}
equipped with
this norm is a Banach space, known
as the
topological dual space
of
V.

Elements of
V^{*}
are called

bounded/continuous linear functionals
on
V.

Proof.

This is a general fact about operator norms (see Proposition 5.4 in
[
14
]
).
∎

Proposition 3.26
.

Let
V
be a vector space. For each
v\in V,
define

\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert_{v}:V^{\vee}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}
by

V^{\vee}\ni\chi\mapsto\lVert\chi\rVert_{v}:=|\chi(v)|.

(3.27)

Then
\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert_{v}
is a seminorm on
V^{\vee}.

Furthermore, for each
\chi\in V^{\vee}\setminus\{0\},
there exists a
v\in V

such that
\chi(v)\neq 0,
i.e. the family of seminorms

\{\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert_{v}\}_{v\in V}
is separating.
The same statement holds for
V^{*}\subseteq V^{\vee}
with the same family.

Proof.

This immediately follows from the definitions.
∎

Definition 3.28
.

Let
V
be a vector space and let

\{\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert_{v}\}_{v\in V}
be the family
of seminorms as in Proposition
3.26
.
The Hausdorff topology generated by this family
as described in Proposition
3.21

is known as the
weak* topology
on
V^{*}.

Proposition 3.29
.

Let
V
be a normed vector space.
For each
\chi\in V^{*},\epsilon>0,n\in{{\mathbb{N}}},
and

\alpha_{n}:\overline{n}\to V,
set

B_{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{n}}(\chi):=\bigcap_{j\in\overline{n}}\Big\{\xi\in V^{*}\;:\;\lVert\chi-\xi\rVert_{\alpha_{n}(j)}<\epsilon\Big\}.

(3.30)

Then

\mathfrak{B}:=\Big\{B_{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{n}}(\chi)\subseteq V^{\vee}\;:\;\epsilon>0,\;n\in{{\mathbb{N}}},\;\alpha_{n}:\overline{n}\to V,\;\chi\in V^{*}\Big\}

(3.31)

is a base for the weak* topology on
V^{*}.

Proof.

This is a general fact for the topology generated by a
separating family of seminorms—see Section 2.4 in
[
34
]
.
∎

In other words, a base for the weak* topology consists of
finite intersections of balls coming from the different seminorms.

Proposition 3.32
.

Let
V
be a vector space. A net
11
11

11

A net is a function whose domain is a directed set
(see Section 11 of
[
50
]
).

\chi:\Theta\to V^{*}

in
V^{*}
converges to an element
\lim\chi\in V^{*}

in the weak* topology if and only if

\lim_{\theta\in\Theta}\big(\chi_{\theta}(v)\big)=(\lim\chi)(v)\qquad\forall\;v\in V.

(3.33)

Proof.

See Tao
[
47
]
or Section 5.4 of Folland
[
14
]
.
∎

Nets are needed here because not all compact Hausdorff spaces
are separable (see Section
4.2
).

Proposition 3.34
.

Let
V
be a vector space and let
V^{*}
denote its topological dual space.
The weak* topology on
V^{*}
is the weakest topology (meaning that it has
the fewest open sets) such that the functions

\begin{split}V^{*}&\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{v}}{{\mathbb{C}}}\\
\chi&\mapsto\chi(v)\end{split}

(3.35)

are continuous for all
v\in V.

Proof.

See Section 3.14 of
[
39
]
.
∎

Theorem 3.36

(Banach-Alaoglu Theorem)
.

Let
V
be a Banach space and let
V^{*}
denote its topological dual space.
The closed unit ball (with respect to the norm on
V^{*}
)
in
V^{*}
is compact with respect to the weak* topology.

Proof.

See Theorem 5.18 in
[
14
]
.
∎

This theorem is surprising because the closed (and bounded) unit ball is compact
with respect to the norm topology if and only if the normed vector space
is finite-dimensional
[
47
]
.

3.3
The spectrum of a commutative
C^{*}
-algebra

It is perhaps not so surprising that one can obtain a
commutative
C^{*}
-algebra from a space as in Section
3.1
.
What is more surprising is that there is a way to go back
from
C^{*}
-algebras to topological spaces.
Although
C^{*}
-algebras have a norm (and hence a topology),
keep in mind that a
C^{*}
-algebra is a
linear
space so
obtaining a non-trivial topology from such an object will require some work.

Definition 3.37
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a commutative Banach algebra.
A
character

on
\mathcal{A}
is a continuous nonzero homomorphism

\chi:\mathcal{A}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}
of Banach algebras, i.e.

\chi
is linear, satisfies
\chi(ab)=\chi(a)\chi(b)
for all
a,b\in{{\mathcal{A}}},

and satisfies the condition that there exists an
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}

for which
\chi(a)\neq 0.

Characters form a subset of
{{\mathcal{A}}}^{*},

the topological dual space of
{{\mathcal{A}}}.
This dual space
has a natural topology coming from
the norms on both
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathbb{C}}},

but the topology of interest for us is the
weak* topology on
{{\mathcal{A}}}^{*}.

Definition 3.38
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a commutative Banach algebra. The

spectrum of
\mathcal{A}
is the set

\sigma(\mathcal{A}):=\left\{\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\chi}{{\mathbb{C}}}\;:\;\chi\text{ is a character}\right\}.

(3.39)

equipped with the subspace topology coming from
\mathcal{A}^{*}

via the weak* topology.

Remark 3.40
.

The relationship between the spectrum of a commutative
C^{*}
-algebra
and the spectrum of a certain operator on a Hilbert space is
described in Proposition 1.15 in
[
13
]
. Briefly, if
A
is bounded and
normal, then the operator-theoretic spectrum of
A
is canonically
homeomorphic with the
spectrum of the commutative unital
*
-subalgebra generated by
A.

This is the closure of the set of polynomials in
A
and
A^{*}.
By definition
of being normal,
A
and
A^{*}
commute so that the order does not matter
in which the
A
’s and
A^{*}
’s appear in such polynomials guaranteeing that
this
C^{*}
-algebra is commutative.

Proposition 3.41
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a Banach algebra and let
\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})
be a character.
Then

i.

\chi(1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}})=1,

ii.

\chi(a)\neq 0
for all invertible elements
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}},
and

iii.

|\chi(a)|\leq\lVert a\rVert
for all
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

Proof.

See Proposition 1.10 in
[
13
]
.
∎

Proposition 3.42
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a commutative Banach algebra.
Then the spectrum

\sigma(\mathcal{A})
is a nonempty compact Hausdorff space.

Proof.

See Theorem 11.9 in
[
39
]
.

\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})
is nonempty because
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is unital.
∎

Proposition 3.43
.

Let
{{\mathcal{B}}}\xrightarrow{f}{{\mathcal{A}}}
be
*
-homomorphism of
commutative Banach algebras. Then the function

\begin{split}\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})&\xrightarrow{\sigma^{f}}\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\\
\chi&\mapsto\chi\circ f\end{split}

(3.44)

is continuous.

Proof.

First, note that since

(\chi\circ f)(xy)=\chi\big(f(x)f(y)\big)=\chi\big(f(x)\big)\chi\big(f(y)\big)=(\chi\circ f)(x)(\chi\circ f)(y)

(3.45)

for all
x,y\in{{\mathcal{B}}},

\chi\circ f
is indeed an element of
\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}}).

We will give two proofs of continuity,
one using open sets and the other using nets.

i.

It suffices to show that the inverse
image of a base element gets sent
to an open set. By Proposition
3.29

and by definition of the subspace topology, a base for the
topology on
\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})
is given by

\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\cap B_{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{n}}(\xi)=\left\{\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\;:\;\lVert\chi-\xi\rVert_{\alpha_{n}(j)}<\epsilon\quad\forall\;j\in\overline{n}\right\}

(3.46)

over all
\xi\in{{\mathcal{B}}}^{*},\alpha_{n}:\overline{n}\to{{\mathcal{B}}},\epsilon>0,n\in{{\mathbb{N}}}.

\sigma^{f}
is continuous if
12
12

12

Let
f:X\to X^{\prime}
be a function
and let
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}

be bases for topologies on
X
and

X^{\prime},
respectively.
f
is continuous
if for every
x\in X,

and for any
B^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
with

f(x)\in B^{\prime},

there exists a
B\in\mathfrak{B}
with
x\in B
such that

f(B)\subseteq B^{\prime}
(this is a simple exercise in the definitions).

for every
\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})
and every
basic set
13
13

13

Technically, we should have said that
for every
\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})
and every
basic set
B
containing
\chi\circ f,

there exists an open set
U
containing

\chi
whose image is contained in
B.

However, due to the seminorms, it suffices
to take the basic set
B
containing

\chi\circ f
to be centered at
\chi\circ f

by choosing it to be sufficiently small.

\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\cap B_{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{n}}(\chi\circ f)

there exists an open set containing

\chi
whose image is contained in this set.
In fact, the basic set
B_{\epsilon}^{f\circ\alpha_{n}}(\chi)

accomplishes this goal because

\lVert\chi-\xi\rVert_{f\circ\alpha_{n}(j)}=\left|\chi\Big(f\big(\alpha_{n}(j)\big)\Big)-\xi\Big(f\big(\alpha_{n}(j)\big)\Big)\right|=\lVert\chi\circ f-\xi\circ f\rVert_{\alpha_{n}(j)},

(3.47)

which shows that

\begin{split}\sigma^{f}\Big(\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\cap B_{\epsilon}^{f\circ\alpha_{n}}(\chi)\Big)&=\Big\{\xi\circ f\in\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\;:\;\lVert\chi-\xi\rVert_{f\circ\alpha_{n}(j)}<\epsilon\quad\forall\;j\in\overline{n}\Big\}\\
&=\Big\{\xi\circ f\in\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\;:\;\lVert\chi\circ f-\xi\circ f\rVert_{\alpha_{n}(j)}<\epsilon\quad\forall\;j\in\overline{n}\Big\}\\
&\subseteq\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\cap B_{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{n}}(\chi\circ f).\end{split}

(3.48)

Thus,
\sigma^{f}
is continuous.

ii.

Let
\chi:\Theta\to\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})
be a net converging to
\lim\chi

(in the weak* topology).
The goal is to show that the net
\sigma^{f}\circ\chi:\Theta\to\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})

converges to
\sigma^{f}(\lim\chi)\equiv(\lim\chi)\circ f.

For every
b\in{{\mathcal{B}}},

\lim_{\theta\in\Theta}\big(\sigma^{f}(\chi_{\theta})(b)\big)=\lim_{\theta\in\Theta}\Big(\chi_{\theta}\big(f(b)\big)\Big)=(\lim\chi)\big(f(b)\big)=\big(\sigma^{f}(\lim\chi)\big)(b),

(3.49)

which establishes the required weak* convergence and thus
shows that
\sigma^{f}
is continuous.

∎

As the reader may have noticed, the proof was much shorter using nets.
The technique of using nets to prove continuity of various functions
will be heavily used in Section
4
.

Proposition 3.50
.

The assignment

\begin{split}\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}&\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathbf{cHaus}\\
{{\mathcal{A}}}&\mapsto\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\\
\Big({{\mathcal{B}}}\xrightarrow{f}{{\mathcal{A}}}\Big)&\mapsto\Big(\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\xleftarrow{\;\sigma^{f}}\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\Big)\end{split}

(3.51)

from Definition
3.38
and
Proposition
3.43

defines a functor.

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to the proof of
Proposition
3.7
.
∎

(3.52)

(3.53)

do

.

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 63.3131pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-63.3131pt\raise 22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.43056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cHaus}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.77779pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Gamma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.06944pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}

(3.54)

indicating a precise sense in which
\sigma
and
C
are close to being inverses
of each other.
A natural isomorphism between functors is analogous to a homotopy
between continuous functions or a unitary intertwiner between representations
and what it amounts to more precisely will
be described presently. This will show that the categories

\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\mathbf{cHaus}
are
equivalent
.

Notation 3.55
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
commutative Banach algebra and set

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}&\xrightarrow{\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}}C\big(\sigma(\mathcal{A})\big)\\
a&\mapsto\Big(\chi\mapsto\big(\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}(a)\big)(\chi):=\chi(a)\Big).\end{split}

(3.56)

\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
is called the

Gelfand transform on
\mathcal{A}
.

Proposition 3.57
.

The following facts are true regarding the Gelfand transform.

i.

For every commutative
Banach algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}},

the Gelfand transform
\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}:{{\mathcal{A}}}\to C_{c}(\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}}))

is a
homomorphism of Banach algebras (preserving units).

ii.

If
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a commutative
C^{*}
-algebra,
then
\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
is a
*
-isomorphism (in particular, it is isometric).

Proof.

See
Theorem 1.13 parts (a) and (d)
and Theorem 1.20 in
[
13
]

for the first and second claims, respectively.
∎

Remark 3.58
.

If
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is just an involutive Banach algebra, then

\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}(a^{*})
is not always equal to

\big(\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}(a)\big)^{*}
for all
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.
Hence,
\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}

is not necessarily a
*
-homomorphism
(see Proposition 1.14 in
[
13
]
).

Proposition 3.59
.

The assignment

\begin{split}\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}_{0}&\xrightarrow{\Gamma}\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}_{1}\\
{{\mathcal{A}}}&\mapsto\Big({{\mathcal{A}}}\xrightarrow{\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}}C\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\big)\Big)\end{split}

(3.60)

from Definition
3.55

defines a natural isomorphism

\Gamma:\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}}\Rightarrow C\circ\sigma.

Proof.

Associated to any
*
-homomorphism
{{\mathcal{B}}}\xrightarrow{f}{{\mathcal{A}}}

of
C^{*}
-algebras is the diagram

3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\big)}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-48.18408pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-37.5pt\raise-16.08334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise (3.61)

To see that it commutes, let
b\in{{\mathcal{B}}}.

Going along the top and right arrows gives

C^{\sigma(f)}\big(\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}(b)\big)=\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}(b)\circ\sigma^{f}

by definition of
C
as a functor
(see Proposition
3.7
).
Going along the left and bottom arrows
gives

\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}\big(f(b)\big).

To see that these two elements of

C\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\big)

are equal, let
\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}}).

Then

which proves that
\Gamma

.
∎

Proposition 3.63
.

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space.
The assignment

\begin{split}h_{X}:X&\to\sigma\big(C(X)\big)\\
x&\mapsto\Big(C(X)\ni f\mapsto\big(h_{X}(x)\big)\big(f\big):=f(x)\Big)\end{split}

(3.64)

is a well-defined homeomorphism.

Proof.

See Theorem 1.16 of
[
13
]
.
and Example 2 after
Theorem 1.30 in
[
13
]
.
∎

Remark 3.65
.

If we had only assumed that
X
was compact and not Hausdorff, then

h_{X}
would not be one-to-one. To see this, let
x,x^{\prime}\in X
be two distinct
points that cannot be separated by open sets. For any continuous function

\varphi:X\to{{\mathbb{C}}},
the value of
\varphi
on these two points is forced to be the same,

\varphi(x)=\varphi(x^{\prime}),
i.e.
h_{X}(x)=h_{X}(x^{\prime}).

Proposition 3.66
.

The assignment

\begin{split}\mathbf{cHaus}_{0}&\xrightarrow{h}\mathbf{cHaus}_{1}\\
X&\mapsto\Big(X\xrightarrow{h_{X}}\sigma\big(C(X)\big)\Big)\end{split}

(3.67)

from Proposition
3.63

defines a natural isomorphism

h:\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{cHaus}}\Rightarrow\sigma\circ C.

Proof.

Associated to any morphism
X\xrightarrow{f}Y

of compact Hausdorff spaces is the diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 48.18408pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-45.03471pt\raise 22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\sigma\big(C(Y)\big)}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-48.18408pt\raise 37.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.38391pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma^{C(f)}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern (3.68)

To see that this diagram commutes, let
x\in X.

Going along the top and right arrows gives

\sigma^{C(f)}\big(h_{X}(x)\big)=h_{X}(x)\circ C^{f}

by definition of
\sigma
as a functor
(see Proposition
3.43
).
Going along the left and bottom arrows gives

h_{Y}\big(f(x)\big).

To see that these two elements of

\sigma\big(C(Y)\big),
i.e.
non-zero characters
C(Y)\to{{\mathbb{C}}},
are equal,
let
\varphi:Y\to{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a continuous function on
Y.

Then

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 87.46349pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.1464pt\raise 38.8321pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern f)(x)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-82.19626pt\raise-14.83247pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big(h_{X}(x)\big)\big(\varphi\circ f\big)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-87.46349pt\raise (3.69)

which proves that
h
is a
natural transformation. It is a natural

iso
morphism by Proposition

3.63
.
∎

3.5
The commutative Gelfand-Naimark equivalence

The following theorem is a categorical phrasing of a theorem
due to Gelfand and Naimark (Lemma 1 in
[
18
]
).
It describes in what sense
the functors
C
and
\sigma
are inverses of each other.
The natural transformations
\Gamma
and
h
also satisfy a universal
property that indicates in what sense they are inverses of each other.

Theorem 3.70
.

Using the notation from above,

\left(\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathbf{cHaus},\mathbf{cHaus}\xrightarrow{C}\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{id}\xRightarrow{\Gamma}C\circ\sigma,\sigma\circ C\xRightarrow{h^{-1}}\mathrm{id}\right)

(3.71)

is an adjoint equivalence of categories.

Proof.

Because of Propositions
3.59

and
3.66
,
it suffices to check the zig-zag identities.
The first one is given by

29.0257pt\raise-7.71619pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.11299pt\raise 23.39168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 7.71619pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.2504pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{C}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.36035pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}

(3.72)

which translates to commutativity of the diagram
14
14

14

The arrows are in the direction required
by contravariance of the functors.

\textstyle{C(X)}
\textstyle{C\Big(\sigma\big(C(X)\big)\Big)}
\textstyle{C(X)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{C(X)}}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{C(X)}}
\scriptstyle{C(h_{X}^{-1})}

(3.73)

for every compact Hausdorff space
X.

Because all morphisms here are invertible,
it is equivalent to show that
the diagram

\textstyle{C(X)}
\textstyle{C\Big(\sigma\big(C(X)\big)\Big)}
\textstyle{C(X)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{C(X)}}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{C(X)}}
\scriptstyle{C(h_{X})}

(3.74)

commutes, which would provide a sense in which
h
is the left inverse of
\Gamma.

Thus, let
\varphi\in C(X).

Applying the composition along the top
two arrows to this element gives

C^{h_{X}}\big(\Gamma_{C(X)}(\varphi)\big)=\Gamma_{C(X)}(\varphi)\circ h_{X}

(3.75)

by Proposition
3.3
. This is a map

X\to\sigma\big(C(X)\big)\to{{\mathbb{C}}}
and is therefore
determined pointwise so let
x\in X
and apply
this map to it. The result is

\big(\Gamma_{C(X)}(\varphi)\big)\big(h_{X}(x)\big)\overset{(\ref{eq:Gelfandtransform})}{=\joinrel=}h_{X}(x)\big(\varphi\big)\overset{(\ref{eq:evaluation})}{=\joinrel=}\varphi(x),

(3.76)

which proves the first zig-zag identity.
The other zig-zag identity

3.0pt\raise-2.77779pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-61.16537pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.43056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cHaus}^{\mathrm{op}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 15.70651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.77779pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 92.43814pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.43056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cHaus}^{\mathrm{op}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-80.72104pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-61.16537pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.07793pt\raise 30.78824pt\raise-6.42934pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.31697pt\raise 22.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 30.78824pt\raise 6.42934pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.62822pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.93056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\sigma}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.36035pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}

(3.77)

follows by a similar calculation. For a commutative
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}},

this identity says that the diagram

\textstyle{\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})}
\textstyle{\sigma\Big(C\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\big)\Big)}
\textstyle{\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})}}
\scriptstyle{h_{\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}})}

(3.78)

commutes, which provides a sense in which
h
is the right inverse of
\Gamma.

∎

The upshot of the Gelfand-Naimark Theorem that we emphasize here
is that it provides one with the perspective that the study of topology
(compact Hausdorff spaces) and their continuous functions
is equivalent to the study of
commutative

C^{*}
-algebras and their
*
-homomorphisms. Therefore,

non
-commutative
C^{*}
-algebras can be interpreted as
non-commutative topology though a more satisfying relationship
to topological concepts is still an area of active research
[
21
]
.
Furthermore, the
*
-homomorphisms between these
non-commutative spaces can be interpreted as deterministic processes.
In the next section, we will describe how to fit in stochastic maps
(non-deterministic processes)
and motivate non-commutative probability theory.

4
Abstract probability theory

The table below collects several of the categories that have been used
along with a few new ones.

Morphisms

\mathbf{FinSetStoch}

continuous functions

\mathbf{cHausStoch}

compact Hausdorff spaces

(continuous) stochastic maps

\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}Alg}

C^{*}
-algebras

-homomorphisms

finite-dimensional commutative

C^{*}

C^{*}
