is not necessarily
continuous for every Borel subset
E\subseteq\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}}),
in general

\mu(E)\neq\lim_{\theta\in\Theta}\sum_{i_{\theta}\in I_{\theta}}\lambda_{i_{\theta}}(E),

(4.143)

where
I_{\theta}
is the subset of
\{1,\dots,N_{\theta}\}
for which

\chi_{i_{\theta}}\in E.

A counterexample is given by the sequence

{{\mathbb{N}}}\ni n\mapsto\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{\frac{k}{n}}

(4.144)

of a uniform sum of Dirac measures on
[0,1].
This sequence
converges to the standard Lebesgue measure
\mu

on
[0,1]
in the vague topology.
However,

\mu_{n}\big({{\mathbb{Q}}}\cap[0,1]\big)=1

while

\mu\big({{\mathbb{Q}}}\cap[0,1]\big)=0.

In the finite-dimensional setting, this does not occur,
nets are not required, and
\omega

is

uniquely expressed as a convex decomposition of extremal states.
The measure of
E
is the sum of the weights corresponding to
restricting this sum to the contributions coming only from
E.

This is because the definition of a Choquet simplex is one that agrees
with the definition of a simplex in finite dimensional vector spaces
(see Proposition 10.10 in
[
35
]
).

Theorem 4.145
.

Let
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be commutative
C^{*}
-algebras and let

f:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a positive (unital) map. For each character

\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}}),
there exists a unique regular probability measure

\sigma^{f}_{\chi}\in\mathrm{ProbMeas}\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\big)

such that

\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\mathrm{ev}_{b}\;d\sigma^{f}_{\chi}=\chi\big(f(b)\big)\qquad\forall\;b\in{{\mathcal{B}}}.

(4.146)

Furthermore, the assignment

\begin{split}\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})&\xrightarrow{\sigma(f)\equiv\sigma^{f}}\mathrm{ProbMeas}\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\big)\\
\chi&\xmapsto{\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\sigma^{f}_{\chi},\end{split}

(4.147)

where
\sigma^{f}_{\chi}
is the unique regular probability measure on
\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})

associated to the state
\chi\circ f\in{{\mathcal{S}}}({{\mathcal{B}}})
via Theorem

4.134
,
defines a stochastic map
\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}}).

Proof.

Since
\chi\circ f
is a state on
{{\mathcal{A}}},
Theorem

4.134

guarantees there exists a unique regular probability measure
\sigma^{f}_{\chi}

satisfying (
4.146
).
The only thing left to check is that this defines a stochastic map,
i.e. that the function
\sigma^{f}:\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\to\mathrm{ProbMeas}\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\big)

is continuous with respect to the vague topology.
Let
\chi:\Theta\to\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})
be a net of characters converging to
\lim\chi.

The goal is to show that

\lim_{\theta\in\Theta}\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\varphi\;d\sigma^{f}_{\chi_{\theta}}=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\varphi\;d\sigma^{f}_{\lim\chi}\qquad\forall\;\varphi\in C\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\big).

(4.148)

The Gelfand transform
\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{B}}}}:{{\mathcal{B}}}\to C\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\big)
sending

b\in{{\mathcal{B}}}
to the evaluation function
\mathrm{ev}_{b}
is a

C^{*}
-algebra isomorphism by Proposition
3.57
.
Hence, every continuous function on the spectrum of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
is
of this form. This fact simplifies the above goal to simply showing that

\lim_{\theta\in\Theta}\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\mathrm{ev}_{b}\;d\sigma^{f}_{\chi_{\theta}}=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\mathrm{ev}_{b}\;d\sigma^{f}_{\lim\chi}\qquad\forall\;b\in{{\mathcal{B}}}.

(4.149)

This follows from the previous results since

\begin{split}\lim_{\theta\in\Theta}\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\mathrm{ev}_{b}\;d\sigma^{f}_{\chi_{\theta}}&=\lim_{\theta\in\Theta}\chi_{\theta}\big(f(b)\big)\quad\text{by (\ref{eq:characterpullsbacktostate})}\\
&=(\lim\chi)\big(f(b)\big)\quad\text{ by weak* convergence of the net $\chi$}\\
&=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\mathrm{ev}_{b}\;d\sigma^{f}_{\lim\chi}\quad\text{by (\ref{eq:characterpullsbacktostate})}\end{split}

(4.150)

for all
b\in{{\mathcal{B}}}.

Therefore
\sigma^{f}
is a stochastic map.
∎

Theorem 4.151
.

The assignment

\begin{split}\mathbf{cC\text{*-}AlgPos}^{\mathrm{op}}&\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathbf{cHausStoch}\\
{{\mathcal{A}}}&\mapsto\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\\
\Big({{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.34204pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{A}}}\Big)&\mapsto\Big(\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 18.76074pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-18.76074pt\raise 7.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma(f)\equiv\sigma^{f}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\Big)\\
\end{split}

(4.152)

from Definition
3.38

and Theorem
4.145

defines a functor, henceforth referred to as the

stochastic spectrum functor
.

Proof.

The identity positive map gets sent to the identity stochastic map
by uniqueness of the measure and the fact that the Dirac measure
is a regular measure satisfying the required conditions.
Let
\mathcal{C},{{\mathcal{B}}},
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be commutative
C^{*}
-algebras and let

f:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
g:\mathcal{C}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{B}}}
be positive maps.
Let
\sigma^{f}:\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\to\mathrm{ProbMeas}\big(\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\big),

\sigma^{g}:\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\to\mathrm{ProbMeas}\big(\sigma(\mathcal{C})\big),

and

\sigma^{f\circ g}:\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\to\mathrm{ProbMeas}\big(\sigma(\mathcal{C})\big)

denote the images of
f,g,
and
f\circ g,
under
\sigma,
respectively.
Let
\sigma^{g}\circ\sigma^{f}:\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\to\mathrm{ProbMeas}\big(\sigma(\mathcal{C})\big)

denote the composition
\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;\sigma(\mathcal{C})
of the stochastic
map
\sigma^{f}
followed by
\sigma^{g}.
The goal is to show

\sigma^{f\circ g}=\sigma^{g}\circ\sigma^{f}.
Since
\sigma^{f\circ g}_{\chi}

and
(\sigma^{g}\circ\sigma^{f})_{\chi}
are regular probability measures for each

\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}}),

by uniqueness in Theorem
4.145
,
it suffices to show

\int\displaylimits_{\sigma(\mathcal{C})}\mathrm{ev}_{c}\;d(\sigma^{g}\circ\sigma^{f})_{\chi}=\chi\Big(f\big(g(c)\big)\Big)

(4.153)

for all
c\in\mathcal{C}
and for all
\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}}).

Therefore, first let
c\in\mathcal{C}
be positive so that
\mathrm{ev}_{c}\geq 0,

and fix
\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}}),
and let

0\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\mathrm{ev}_{c}

(4.154)

be a sequence
s:{{\mathbb{N}}}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}^{\sigma(\mathcal{C})}
of simple functions converging to

\mathrm{ev}_{c}
and satisfying all the conditions as in
Lemma
4.10
.
Then,

\begin{split}\chi\Big(f\big(g(c)\big)\Big)&=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\mathrm{ev}_{g(c)}(\psi)\;d\sigma^{f}_{\chi}(\psi)\quad\text{by definition of $\sigma^{f}_{\chi}$}\\
&=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\psi\big(g(c)\big)\;d\sigma^{f}_{\chi}(\psi)\quad\text{by definition of $\mathrm{ev}_{g(c)}$}\\
&=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\left(\;\int\displaylimits_{\sigma(\mathcal{C})}\mathrm{ev}_{c}\;d\sigma^{g}_{\psi}\right)d\sigma^{f}_{\chi}(\psi)\quad\text{by definition of $\sigma^{g}_{\psi}$}\\
&=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i_{n}}s_{n,i_{n}}\sigma^{g}_{\psi}(E_{n,i_{n}})\;d\sigma^{f}_{\chi}(\psi)\quad\text{by definition of Lebesgue integral}\\
&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i_{n}}s_{n,i_{n}}\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\sigma^{g}_{\psi}(E_{n,i_{n}})\;d\sigma^{f}_{\chi}(\psi)\quad\text{by Monotone Convergence Theorem}\\
&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i_{n}}s_{n,i_{n}}(\sigma^{g}\circ\sigma^{f})_{\chi}(E_{n,i_{n}})\quad\text{by definition of $(\sigma^{g}\circ\sigma^{f})_{\chi}$}\\
&=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma(\mathcal{C})}\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i_{n}}s_{n,i_{n}}\chi_{E_{n,i_{n}}}\;d(\sigma^{g}\circ\sigma^{f})_{\chi}\quad\text{by definition of Lebesgue integral}\\
&=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma(\mathcal{C})}\mathrm{ev}_{c}\;d(\sigma^{g}\circ\sigma^{f})_{\chi}\quad\text{by definition of $s$.}\end{split}

(4.155)

Again, the Monotone Convergence Theorem applies by similar
arguments as in step iii. in the proof of Proposition
4.79
.
The case of more general
c\in\mathcal{C}
for which
\mathrm{ev}_{c}
need
not be positive is handled by splitting up
\mathrm{ev}_{c}

using a Jordan decomposition and applying a similar procedure.
∎

Example 4.156
.

\rho:[-1,1]\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
be the restriction of the Gaussian used to describe

of heat at the origin, namely

[-1,1]\ni x\mapsto\rho(x):=\frac{\exp\left(-100x^{2}\right)}{\int_{-1}^{1}\exp\left(-100y^{2}\right)dy},

(4.158)

Figure 5:
The curve drawn in red is the graph of
\rho
in (
4.157
)
(cut off from above to fit on the page)
while the (larger) blue bullets represent the heights of the Dirac point distributions.
The heights for the point measures decrease because the area over which
they are used to approximate functions decreases as
n
increases.

Although the sequence of measures
d\mu_{n}
does not converge
to
\rho\;\!d\mu_{\text{Lebesgue}}
in the total variation norm topology,
the convergence holds in the vague topology, i.e.

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi\;d\mu_{n}=\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi(x)\rho(x)\;dx\qquad\forall\;\varphi\in C\big([-1,1]\big).

(4.159)

This gives an example of a probability measure on
[-1,1]
that can be
approximated by a sequence of convex sums of Dirac point measures
with respect to the vague topology. Again, it is not true that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(E)=\int_{E}\rho(x)\;dx
for all Borel measurable
sets
E.
For example, take
E:=[-1,1]\cap({{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus{{\mathbb{Q}}}),
all irrationals
in
[-1,1].
The term on the left is
0
while the term on the right is
1.

4.6
The stochastic commutative Gelfand-Naimark Theorem

Notice that when
{{\mathcal{A}}}=C(X)
with
X
a compact Hausdorff space and

\omega\in{{\mathcal{S}}}\big(C(X)\big),
Theorem

4.134

says that there exists a unique regular probability measure
\mu

on
\sigma\big(C(X)\big)
such that

\int\displaylimits_{\sigma\big(C(X)\big)}\mathrm{ev}_{\varphi}\;d\mu=\omega(\varphi)\qquad\forall\;\varphi\in C(X).

(4.160)

Meanwhile, by the RMK Representation Theorem, there exists a unique regular
probability measure
\nu
on
X
such that

\omega(\varphi)=\int_{X}\varphi\;d\nu\qquad\forall\;\varphi\in C(X).

(4.161)

By the Gelfand-Naimark Theorem, we know that the spaces

X
and
\sigma\big(C(X)\big)
are homeomorphic and one expects
these two measures to be related.
In fact, the function
h_{X}:X\to\sigma\big(C(X)\big)

pushes forward the measure
\nu
to
\mu
and the inverse

h_{X}^{-1}:\sigma\big(C(X)\big)\to X
pushes forward the measure
\mu

to
\nu.

This is explained in more detail in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.162
.

The stochastic spectrum functor together with the
natural isomorphisms

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 84.86858pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-84.86858pt\raise 22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cHausStoch}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 20.13142pt\raise 22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cHausStoch}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-36.23735pt\raise-22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.77779pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}AlgPos}^{\mathrm{op}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.37843pt\raise-5.39166pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{C}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 84.28593pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-84.28593pt\raise 22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.77779pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}AlgPos}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 20.71407pt\raise 7.55301pt\raise-16.02779pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-37.6044pt\raise-5.39166pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.34029pt\raise 27.93056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.71405pt\raise 22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.9602pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Gamma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.02779pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}

(4.163)

from the commutative Gelfand-Naimark Theorem
form an adjoint equivalence of categories.

Proof.

The only thing that has not been shown is that
the transformations
\Gamma
and
h
still satisfy naturality.
Note that each is an isomorphism in its respective category
on objects due to the usual Gelfand-Naimark Theorem.

i.

Proof that
h
is natural.

Let
\mu:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y
be a stochastic map and let

\sigma^{C(\mu)}\!:\!\sigma\big(C(X)\big)\!\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;\!\sigma\big(C(Y)\big)

denote the stochastic map obtained from applying the functor
C

and then
\sigma
to
\mu.

The goal is to prove that the diagram

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\sigma(C(X))}
\textstyle{\sigma(C(Y))}
\scriptstyle{h_{X}}
\scriptstyle{h_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{C(\mu)}}

(4.164)

commutes. Note that for any Borel set
E\in\mathcal{M}_{\sigma(C(Y))}

and for any
x\in X,

the lower left composition
\delta_{h_{Y}}\circ\mu
applied to

x
evaluated at
E
gives

(\delta_{h_{Y}}\circ\mu)_{x}(E)=\int_{Y}\delta_{h_{Y}(y)}(E)\;d\mu_{x}(y)=\int_{Y}\chi_{h_{Y}^{-1}(E)}(y)\;d\mu_{x}(y)=\mu_{x}\big(h_{Y}^{-1}(E)\big).

(4.165)

In other words,
(\delta_{h_{Y}}\circ\mu)_{x}
is the pushforward of the measure

\mu_{x}
along the function
h_{Y}.
Now we check that this equals

\big(\sigma^{C(\mu)}\circ h_{X}\big)_{x}
for every
x\in X
by integrating

\mathrm{ev}_{E}
with respect to this pushforward measure and

\varphi\in C(Y).

By the change of variables
formula for integrating measurable functions with respect to
pushforward measures (see Theorem 3.6.1 in
[
7
]
),

\int\displaylimits_{\sigma(C(Y))}\mathrm{ev}_{\varphi}\;d(\delta_{h_{Y}}\circ\mu)_{x}=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma(C(Y))}\mathrm{ev}_{\varphi}\;d\big(\mu_{x}\circ h_{Y}^{-1}\big)=\int_{Y}(\mathrm{ev}_{\varphi}\circ h_{Y})\;d\mu_{x}

(4.166)

since
\mathrm{ev}_{\varphi}
is continuous (and hence measurable).
Therefore,

\begin{split}\int\displaylimits_{\sigma(C(Y))}\mathrm{ev}_{\varphi}\;d(\delta_{h_{Y}}\circ\mu)_{x}&=\int_{Y}\mathrm{ev}_{\varphi}\big(h_{Y}(y)\big)\;d\mu_{x}(y)\quad\text{ by (\ref{eq:stochasticgelfandnatural})}\\
&=\int_{Y}h_{Y}(y)\big(\varphi\big)\;d\mu_{x}(y)\quad\text{ by definition of $\mathrm{ev}_{\varphi}$}\\
&=\int_{Y}\varphi(y)\;d\mu_{x}(y)\quad\text{ by definition of $h_{Y}$ (\ref{eq:evaluation})}\\
&=\big(C^{\mu}(\varphi)\big)(x)\quad\text{ by definition of $C^{\mu}$ (\ref{eq:curlyEonstochasticmaps})}\\
&=h_{X}(x)\big(C^{\mu}(\varphi)\big)\quad\text{ by definition of $h_{X}$ (\ref{eq:evaluation})}\\
&=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma(C(Y))}\mathrm{ev}_{\varphi}\;d\sigma^{C(\mu)}_{h_{X}(x)}\quad\text{ by definition of $\sigma^{C(\mu)}_{h_{X}(x)}$.}\end{split}

(4.167)

Since this is true for all
\varphi\in C(Y),
the uniqueness of measures on
\sigma(C(Y))

from Theorem
4.134
implies

\sigma^{C(\mu)}_{h_{X}(x)}=(\delta_{h_{Y}}\circ\mu)_{x}
for all
x\in X.

Therefore, the diagram above commutes
and
h:\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{cHausStoch}}\Rightarrow\sigma\circ C
is a natural isomorphism.

ii.

Proof that
\Gamma
is natural.

Let
f:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a positive map. The goal is to show that the
diagram

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\textstyle{C(\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}}))}
\textstyle{C(\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}}))}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{B}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{C^{\sigma(f)}}

(4.168)

commutes, i.e. for arbitrary
b\in{{\mathcal{B}}},
the two functions

C^{\sigma(f)}\big(\Gamma_{B}(b)\big)
and

\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}\big(f(a)\big)
are equal on
\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}}).

To check equality of these functions, evaluating them on an arbitrary

\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})
gives

\Big(C^{\sigma(f)}\big(\Gamma_{B}(b)\big)\Big)(\chi)=\int\displaylimits_{\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})}\mathrm{ev}_{b}\;d\sigma^{f}_{\chi}=\chi\big(f(b)\big)=\Big(\Gamma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}\big(f(a)\big)\Big)(\chi),

(4.169)

which is the desired result.

∎

We should also check that this equivalence agrees with the
usual Gelfand-Naimark equivalence for deterministic processes,
i.e. for
*
-homomorphisms on the
C^{*}
-algebra side and
measure-preserving functions on the analytic side.

Theorem 4.170
.

Let
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be commutative
C^{*}
-algebras, let

f:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a
*
-homomorphism, and let
\chi\in\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})

be a character on
{{\mathcal{A}}}.

Then the corresponding regular probability measure
\sigma^{f}_{\chi}

is the Dirac measure at
\chi\circ f,
i.e.
\sigma^{f}_{\chi}=\delta_{\chi\circ f}.

Therefore,
\sigma(f)
uniquely determines
a continuous function
\sigma({{\mathcal{A}}})\to\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})

agreeing with the usual spectrum functor on
\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg},
i.e. the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbf{cHaus}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{cHausStoch}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}AlgPos}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\delta}

(4.171)

commutes.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\chi\circ f
is a character on
\sigma({{\mathcal{B}}})

and that it immediately satisfies the conditions of Theorem

4.145

so that the measure is uniquely specified.
∎

Remark 4.172
.

The full power of Theorem

4.145

is not needed to prove this last result. It follows from a simpler result
due to Bauer (see Proposition 1.4 in
[
35
]
).

5
Closing discussion

We briefly discuss the broad physical scope of our results in the context
of quantum mechanics and then the relationship to similar
conclusions by other authors.

5.1
Categories of classical and quantum dynamics

In this paper, we have collected functors

\textstyle{\mathbf{cHaus}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{cHausStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}AlgPos}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{FinSetFun}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{FinSetStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}Alg}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}AlgPos}}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}

\scriptstyle{C}

\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\delta}

\scriptstyle{\delta}

(5.1)

between a variety of categories. The left face of the cube
consists of dynamics on classical systems (phase space).
The right face describes the algebraic counterparts in terms of observables
of those classical systems (functions on phase space) and how those evolve
under the dynamics. The top face describes
deterministic dynamics in both space and algebraic settings.
The bottom face describes stochastic dynamics
in both space and algebraic settings.
The front face describes finite classical systems (systems whose phase
space is finite).
The back face describes classical systems whose phase space is
possibly infinite.
The functors between the left face and the right face are all equivalences.
The equivalences on the top face are due to Gelfand and Naimark
while the back edge of the bottom face has been described in this work.

This cube has a natural extension to the right for
arbitrary
C^{*}
-algebras.
*
-homomorphisms are therefore
non-commutative analogues of deterministic processes
while completely positive maps are non-commutative analogues of
non-deterministic processes.

\textstyle{\mathbf{cHaus}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{cHausStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}Alg}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{cC\text{*-}AlgPos}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{FinSetFun}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{FinSetStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}Alg}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{fdcC\text{*-}AlgPos}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C\text{*-}Alg}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C\text{*-}AlgCP}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{fdC\text{*-}Alg}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{fdC\text{*-}AlgCP}}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}

\scriptstyle{C}

\scriptstyle{\sigma}

(5.2)

The theorems presented in this work offer more motivation
for thinking of completely positive maps and the dynamics
associated with them in quantum theory as a natural extension of
stochastic dynamics in classical theory.
This can be seen more concretely if one adds more data to these categories.
The categories above only describe dynamics of the observables of a
system, but not of individual states. In other words, we have so far
described the Heisenberg picture of quantum mechanics.
However, this is a red herring. Evolution of states is also described
in this setting because a state
is
a completely positive map.
If a completely positive map
f:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{A}}}
describes the evolution of
observables described by
{{\mathcal{A}}}
to observables described by
{{\mathcal{B}}},

and
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a state on
{{\mathcal{A}}},
then
\omega
evolves to

\omega\circ f:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}
under the evolution described by
f.

Therefore, one can include
both
evolution of observables
and

states by taking
the slice category of
\mathbf{C\text{*-}AlgCP}
over
{{\mathbb{C}}},
denoted by

\mathbf{C\text{*-}AlgCP}/_{{{\mathbb{C}}}}.
The objects of this category are

C^{*}
-algebras equipped with states
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}.

A morphism from
\xi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}
to
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathbb{C}}}
consists of a
completely positive map
f:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;{{\mathcal{A}}}
such that
\xi=\omega\circ f.

This is a non-commutative generalization of spaces equipped with
probability measures.
This situation was described in Example
2.93

though more general dynamics is encompassed in Lindblad’s formalism
[
27
]
.

5.2
Categorical probability theory

As mentioned earlier, Sections
2
and

3
were entirely a review though phrased in
a categorical setting that was used to state and prove various results in
Section
4
.
Many of the results in Section
4

relied on well known facts in analysis though our categorical framework
brought some questions to surface which seem to have not been
addressed earlier.
Similar results were obtained by
Furber and Jacobs
[
15
]
, who
proved an equivalence
of categories between
\mathbf{cHausStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\mathbf{cC\text{*-}AlgPos}
by
realizing the category
\mathbf{cHausStoch}

as the Kleisli category of a particular monad, a slight variant of the Giry monad,
on the category
\mathbf{cHaus}.

There are three main differences between our findings.

i.

Rather than constructing the Giry monad, we worked directly with the
objects and morphisms of
\mathbf{cHausStoch}
bypassing the monad entirely.
This saves us quite a bit of space from explaining background material
on monads, Kleisli categories, and how these are related to adjunctions,
which is all beautifully explained in
[
42
]
.
Furthermore, it makes our presentation more accessible to those with a
minimal categorical background.
However, as a result, some proofs become a bit more
involved compared to
[
15
]
. For instance, given two stochastic maps

\mu:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y
and
\nu:Y\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Z,

proving that the composition
\nu\circ\mu
is well-defined,
continuous, regular, and a Markov kernel was quite involved
(see Proposition
4.79
and the preceding lemmas).
From the Giry monad perspective,
\mathrm{ProbMeas}
can
be viewed as a functor from the category
\mathbf{cHaus}
to itself (one must prove this).
In addition, there are two natural transformations that provide

\mathrm{ProbMeas}
with the structure of a monad.
The inclusion of a space
X
into
\mathrm{ProbMeas}(X)
via
the Dirac measure defines a natural transformation

\delta:\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{cHaus}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{ProbMeas}.

In addition, there is a natural transformation

b:\mathrm{ProbMeas}\circ\mathrm{ProbMeas}\Rightarrow\mathrm{ProbMeas}

given on a space
X
by the assignment

\begin{split}\mathrm{ProbMeas}\big(\mathrm{ProbMeas}(X)\big)&\xrightarrow{b_{X}}\mathrm{ProbMeas}(X)\\
\Omega&\mapsto\left(\mathcal{M}_{X}\ni E\xmapsto{b_{X}(\Omega)}\hskip-8.53581pt\int\displaylimits_{\mathrm{ProbMeas}(X)}\hskip-14.22636pt\mathrm{ev}_{E}\;d\Omega\right).\end{split}

(5.3)

Notice that one must still show that
\mathrm{ev}_{E}
is measurable
to define this map.

b_{X}
is surprisingly easy to interpret and has significant physical meaning.

\mathrm{ProbMeas}(X)
is a convex subset of the vector space of all
finite measures.
A probability measure on it therefore has a barycenter,
its center of mass.
The map
b_{X}
is precisely the assignment of that probability measure to
its barycenter.
This follows from the fact that for any Borel set
E\in\mathcal{M}_{X},

\int\displaylimits_{\mathrm{ProbMeas}(X)}\hskip-11.38109pt\mathrm{ev}_{E}\;d\Omega\quad=\int\displaylimits_{\mathrm{ProbMeas}(X)}\hskip-17.07164pt\mathrm{ev}_{E}(\mu)\;d\Omega(\mu)\quad=\int\displaylimits_{\mathrm{ProbMeas}(X)}\hskip-17.07164pt\mu(E)\;d\Omega(\mu).

(5.4)

A physically relevant instance where the barycenter naturally occurs
will be discussed after point iii.
Going back to the Giry monad and
\mathbf{cHausStoch}
, using the barycenter map,
the definition of the composition

\nu\circ\mu
can then be taken to be the
composition of the functions

X\xrightarrow{\mu}\mathrm{ProbMeas}(Y)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ProbMeas}(\nu)}\mathrm{ProbMeas}\big(\mathrm{ProbMeas}(Z)\big)\xrightarrow{b_{Z}}\mathrm{ProbMeas}(Z)

(5.5)

from which it immediately follows that the composition is continuous, since
each function in this composition is continuous.
The relationship between our formula for the composition and this composition
of functions follows from the change of variables formula
since applying the above composition to a point gives

X\ni x\mapsto\mu_{x}\mapsto\nu_{*}\mu_{x}\mapsto b_{Z}(\nu_{*}\mu_{x}),

(5.6)

where
\nu_{*}(\mu_{x})
denotes the pushforward measure of
\mu_{x}

along the (measurable) function
\nu:Y\to\mathrm{ProbMeas}(Z).

This evaluates on measurable sets
E\in\mathcal{M}_{Z}
to

\big(b_{Z}(\nu_{*}\mu_{x})\big)(E)=\int\displaylimits_{\mathrm{ProbMeas}(Z)}\hskip-17.07164pt\mathrm{ev}_{E}\;d(\nu_{*}\mu_{x})=\int_{Y}(\mathrm{ev}_{E}\circ\nu)\;d\mu_{x},

(5.7)

which agrees with our definition of composition
(
4.80
).
By using an intermediate amount of
category theory, our work offers a bridge
towards more categorical approaches to probability theory
[
19
]
.

ii.

In
[
15
]
, Furber and Jacobs use the RMK Representation Theorem immediately
so that a stochastic map
X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\;Y
is defined to be a continuous function

X\to{{\mathcal{S}}}\big(C(Y)\big)
into states on the
C^{*}
-algebra of continuous
functions on
Y
instead of a continuous function

X\to\mathrm{ProbMeas}(Y)
into regular probability measures on
Y

(this is how the Kleisli category associated to their version of the
Giry monad was defined).
There is no significant difference between these perspectives. However,
we prefer to think of the left side of our cube in Section

5.1
as purely analytical, topological, and
measure-theoretic. In particular, we provided an explicit
definition of the composition using Markov kernels and without
any reference to the RMK theorem.
The right face of this cube is more algebraic and utilizes algebras of
function spaces, states, positive maps, etc.
Of course, the point of the equivalence between these two faces
is that this distinction is just psychological. Nevertheless, it is
helpful to distinguish the different tools used in both subjects.
As a result, many of the proofs in our paper are completely different in nature
from those of Furber and Jacobs.

iii.

Finally, Furber and Jacobs showed that

C:\mathbf{cHausStoch}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{cC\text{*-}AlgPos}
is
fully faithful and essentially surjective
[
15
]
.
However,
any abstract nonsense construction of an explicit inverse will necessarily
relate any commutative
C^{*}
-algebra to one
of continuous functions on some space and will therefore use the
RMK theorem in its construction.
We have therefore provided an explicit construction of an inverse
without using the Gelfand transform nor the RMK theorem.
Furthermore, we showed that the usual Gelfand transform is
part of the adjoint equivalence for the stochastic Gelfand-Naimark Theorem.
Our construction of the stochastic spectrum functor applies to
positive maps and take characters to states. Choquet theory
was used to “spread” this state out onto the spectrum in terms
of a probability measure which was obtained as a limit of
successive approximations via decompositions of the state into convex
combinations of Dirac measures supported on the space of characters.
This perspective may be useful for
explorations in the category of non-commutative
C^{*}
-algebras.

One instance where the barycenter map is important in physics occurs in
preparing systems on which to perform experiments.
In an experiment, one has a protocol for preparing a state, which will
then be probed in some way, say, by measuring some observable.
Because the probe may alter the state,
one needs the protocol to be as accurate as possible so that the
same state can be constructed so that it can be probed in subsequent experiments.
Indeed, this is how a state is defined mathematically—via its expectation
values.
However, in practice, protocols, as well as instruments, are rarely
perfect. As a result, the prepared states may differ slightly from one another
so that they are more accurately represented by a probability measure
on the set of states. Hence, an experimentalist is not always obtaining
expectation values for the same state. Nevertheless,
after viewing the set of states as probability measures via the RMK
Representation Theorem,
the barycenter map provides one with a canonical state that
represents the average state prepared by the protocol.

This
is the state very often referred to in most textbooks,
but one should keep in mind that the barycenter represents only one part
of all the data.

5.3
The Baire approach

One may have circumvented many of the difficulties we encountered by using
Baire sets instead of Borel sets. This perspective is emphasized in works
such as
[
12
]
.
In this case, one has the following analogue of
Lemma
4.64
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
Y
be a compact Hausdorff space,
let
E\subseteq Y
be a Baire set,
and let
\mathrm{Pr}(Y)
denote all probability measures
on
Y.
Then the evaluation function

\begin{split}\mathrm{Pr}(Y)&\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{E}}{{\mathbb{R}}}\\
\mu&\mapsto\mu(E)\end{split}

(5.9)

is Borel measurable.

Proof.

Let
P
be the collection of all compact
G_{\delta}
subsets of
Y

and let

L:=\left\{E\subseteq Y\;:\;\mathrm{Pr}(Y)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{E}}[0,1]\text{ is Borel measurable}\right\}

(5.10)

i.

The intersection of compact sets is compact and
the finite intersection of a countable collection of open sets is
still a countable intersection
of open sets. Hence
P
is a
\pi
-system.

ii.

This is the same as in the proof of
Lemma
4.64
so
L
is a
\lambda
-system.

iii.

Let
K=U_{1}\cap U_{2}\cap U_{3}\cap\cdots
be a compact
G_{\delta}
set in

Y
with
\{U_{n}\}
an at most countable collection of open sets in
Y.

It suffices to assume that

U_{1}\supseteq U_{2}\supseteq U_{3}\supseteq\cdots

since if this were not the case, the sequence
U_{1},U_{1}\cap U_{2},U_{1}\cap U_{2}\cap U_{3},\dots
would also have intersection equal to
K.

For each
n,
by Urysohn’s Lemma, there exists
a continuous function
f_{n}:Y\to[0,1]
such that
f_{n}(y)=1
for
y\in K
and

f_{n}(y)=0
for
y\in U_{n}^{c}.
Similarly, it suffices to assume that

f_{1}\geq f_{2}\geq f_{3}\geq\cdots
since if this were not the case,
the sequence

{{\mathbb{N}}}\times Y\ni(n,y)\mapsto\min\big\{f_{1}(y),f_{2}(y),\dots,f_{n}(y)\big\}

would also have the same pointwise limit. From these assumptions, it
follows that

\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(y)=\chi_{K}(y)\qquad\forall\;y\in Y.

(5.11)

Hence, for any
a\in{{\mathbb{R}}},
the preimage of
(a,\infty)
under
\mathrm{ev}_{K}

is

\begin{split}\mathrm{ev}_{K}^{-1}\big((a,\infty)\big)&=\big\{\mu\in\mathrm{Pr}(Y)\;:\;\mu(K)>a\big\}\\
&=\left\{\mu\in\mathrm{Pr}(Y)\;:\;\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Y}f_{n}\;d\mu>a\right\}\\
&\qquad\text{by the Monotone Convergence Theorem}\\
&=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\left\{\mu\in\mathrm{Pr}(Y)\;:\;\int_{Y}f_{n}\;d\mu>a\right\}\quad\text{since $\cdots\geq f_{n}\geq f_{n+1}\geq\cdots$}.\end{split}

(5.12)

By definition of the vague topology, the set in curly brackets in the last equality
is open. Hence, this is a countable intersection of open sets and is therefore
a Borel subset of
\mathrm{Pr}(Y).

This shows that
\mathrm{ev}_{K}
is Borel measurable for all
compact
G_{\delta}
sets
K.

Since all the conditions of Dynkin’s
\pi
-
\lambda
theorem are
satisfied, the sigma algebra generated by
P,

which is the Baire
\sigma
-algebra on
Y,
is contained
L.

∎

This allows one to define the composition
of stochastic maps on Baire sets. One can then use the theorem that
every Baire measure extends uniquely to a regular measure
under these assumptions (see Corollary 7.3.4. in
[
8
]
).
However, this would not guarantee that formula
(
4.80
) for the composition of stochastic
maps is still a valid mathematical expression. The construction
of the regular Borel extension of this measure is somewhat formal
and does not provide one with the explicit formula we have produced.
